## 🌙 AUTONOMOUS SESSION 82 (2026-09-12, continued) — RESOLVED: John Henry Keown's Civil War service confirmed via primary NPS record -- Union Corporal, 21st Missouri Infantry (not Georgia/Confederate as previously assumed)

Reopened the long-standing question flagged since session 34 (John Henry Keown, abt 1844-1862, whose death location "Warren, Georgia" suggested a possible Civil War connection). That profile shows the family's actual movements: still in Ohio County, Kentucky as of the 1850 census, but **relocated to Adair County, Missouri by 1853** (sister Nancy E. Keown born there that year) and confirmed in the 1860 census at **Clay Township, Wilmothville, Adair Co, Missouri** -- resolving session 75's tentative, not-adopted "different county, probably unrelated" Missouri lead from that session as, in fact, the correct family after all.

**The key find**: this profile cites **"U.S., Civil War Soldiers, 1861-1865"** (National Park Service's official Civil War Soldiers and Sailors System database) as a source. Pulled that record directly:

> Name: **John H. Keown**
> Side: **Union**
> Regiment State/Origin: **Missouri**
> Regiment: **21st Regiment, Missouri Infantry**
> Company: **B**
> Rank In: **Corporal**
> Rank Out: **Corporal**

**This is a definitive, primary-source Civil War service record** -- John Henry Keown served as a Corporal in Company B, 21st Missouri Infantry (Union Army). The 21st Missouri Infantry saw combat at the Battle of Shiloh in April 1862, among other engagements of that year; his 1862 death (with the other tree's own title noting "dies of sickness") is entirely consistent with disease claiming soldiers during or after that campaign, which killed far more Civil War soldiers than combat did. **The original tree's "Warren, Georgia" death location appears to be an error or refers to something else entirely -- the real, sourced story is Union service with a Missouri regiment**, not a Georgia/Confederate connection as the flag's original wording speculated.

Keown (1853-1879, born in Missouri)** — with the family's Kentucky-to-Missouri move now dated to between 1850 and 1853.

## 🌙 AUTONOMOUS SESSION 86 (2026-09-12, continued) — MAJOR FIND: a FOURTH Revolutionary War Patriot, with an exceptionally detailed pension-documented combat record (Fort Stanwix, Saratoga, Stony Point)

Follow-up to session 85's lead: checked Find A Grave for **Corp. Moses Rawson (1753-1833)**, Silas Rawson's half-brother (both sons of Nathaniel Rawson Jr., Moses via 2nd wife Rachel Daniels, Silas via 1st wife Mary Thwing). Found his memorial (findagrave.com/memorial/85225694) -- also flagged as a **VETERAN**, with an exceptionally rich, well-documented service record:

> "Moses was a Veteran of the American Revolution -- Corporal Moses Rawson fought four and one-half years in the American Revolution and was with 'Mad Anthony Wayne' at Stony Point. According to Revolutionary War Pension Files at the National Archives, he enlisted in the Ninth Regiment of the Massachusetts Line in April, 1777 and was discharged at Camp West Point on April 21, 1780. He participated in the siege of Fort Stanwix, and the battles of Stony Point and Saratoga."

**This is the fourth confirmed Revolutionary War Patriot ancestor found in this entire research push** (after Deacon Seth Strong, Lieut. Oliver Babcock Jr., and Nathaniel Rawson Jr. himself, session 85), and by far the most specifically documented -- actual National Archives pension file records naming his exact regiment (9th Massachusetts Line), enlistment date (April 1777), discharge date and place (Camp West Point, 21 Apr 1780), and three specific, historically significant engagements:
- **Siege of Fort Stanwix** (1777) -- part of the Saratoga campaign, a pivotal defense that helped block British General Barry St. Leger's advance down the Mohawk Valley.
- **Battle of Saratoga** (1777) -- widely regarded as the war's turning point, the American victory that convinced France to formally ally with the United States.
- **Battle of Stony Point** (1779) -- General "Mad Anthony" Wayne's celebrated night bayonet assault, one of the most daring and successful American operations of the war.

The memorial explicitly confirms the family relationship: "**Moses-5 was the son of Nathaniel-4 Rawson (1716-1803)**" (matching session 85's Nathaniel Rawson Jr. exactly) and lists half-sibling "**Silas Rawson Sr, 1739-1819**" -- confirming Moses and Silas (Robert's direct ancestor) as half-brothers. The full published generation chain given here is: **Moses-5 Rawson, Nathaniel-4 Rawson, Nathaniel-3 Rawson, William-2 Rawson, Edward-1 Rawson** -- independently corroborating the entire confirmed line back to Edward Rawson, Secretary of the Massachusetts Bay Colony (session 79), from a completely different source (a dedicated Rawson family archivist's research, credited to "Alison Franks, 2013" and "Mary E. Warner, 2013").

**Newly confirmed facts about Nathaniel Rawson I**: his wife's full identity -- **Hannah Thompson, daughter of Samuel Thompson of Braintree**, married 1712 (not previously documented); that the couple settled in Mendon, Mass.; his civic offices (Selectman 1727-28 and 1730, Assessor 1728, church-building committee 1729); and his exact death date, **April 20, 1731**. Aug 11, 1721), Edward (b. April 19, 1724), and Rachael (b. May 20, 1727) -- Barnabas, Edward, and this Rachael (distinct from the later Rachael in Nathaniel Jr.'s own family) are all newly documented siblings of Nathaniel Rawson Jr., not previously in the tree.

**Then pushed one generation further still** by tracing Nathaniel Rawson I's own cross-reference back to the very start of the book's "SECOND GENERATION" chapter (HathiTrust seq 34, early in the chapter), and found **William Rawson's own full entry** -- Edward Rawson's son, one full generation earlier than anything previously documented in this line:

> "William Rawson² (Edward¹), third son of Edward, was educated to a mercantile life, and became a prominent merchant and an importer of foreign goods. Up to the time of his marriage, in 1673, he resided with his father in Rawson's Lane, now Bromfield Street, Boston, where he kept a dry goods store. At the age of 22 years he was married to Anne Glover, only daughter of Mr. Nathaniel and Mary (Smith) Glover of Dorchester, Mass... on ye 11th day of July, 1673... It seems they were faithful to the great and first command given to man -- to be fruitful and multiply -- for in the space of twenty-five years they had twenty children. Only five sons of them, however, living to grow up and have families of their own. He purchased a house of Mr. John Glover of Boston (an uncle of his wife)... In 1689, he sold his estate back to Mr. Glover... and removed with his family to Dorchester... He afterwards purchased of the heirs of his great uncle, the Rev. John Wilson, a tract of land situated in Braintree... which he made into a homestead, and which is now known as the 'Ancient Rawson Farm.' ... It has been passed down from father to son, unto the fifth generation. From William to his son David, who left it to his son Jonathan in 1760, then to Jonathan, Jr., and his sister Mary, in 1782..."

**This is a major, fully sourced extension of the confirmed direct line**: **Edward Rawson (Secretary of the Massachusetts Bay Colony, 1650-1686) → William Rawson (b. abt. 1651, third son, Boston merchant, m. Anne Glover of Dorchester, 11 July 1673) → Nathaniel Rawson I (1689-1731, m. Hannah Thompson 1712, Mendon selectman) → Nathaniel Rawson Jr. (1716-1803, Revolutionary War Minuteman) → Silas Rawson (1739-1819) → Rev. Daniel Rawson → Anson George Rawson → ... → Robert.** William Rawson is now the earliest-documented Rawson ancestor with a full personal biography (occupation, marriage, residence history, and a named family property, the "Ancient Rawson Farm" in Braintree, Mass., traced through five generations of his descendants) -- one full generation before reaching immigrant-ancestor Edward Rawson himself, who needs no further generations back since he is already the confirmed immigrant/colonial-founder terminus of this line.

## 🌙 AUTONOMOUS SESSION 92 (2026-09-12, continued) — MAJOR FIND: Frances Isbell's line traced back TWO more generations to a Revolutionary War soldier killed in action at the Battle of Stillwater (Saratoga campaign), plus an English immigrant-name-origin legend

Followed up on a genuinely unexplored lead first flagged around session 53-54 and never chased: *Genealogy of the Isbell Family* by Mary Isbell Scott (1929), covering Frances Isbell's own line (Frances being Anson George Rawson's wife, already in Robert's tree with parents Joel Isbell + Mary Chatfield, sourced but not pushed further back). Found the book on HathiTrust (wu.89066154436, 270 pages, full view) and searched "Joel Chatfield" -- immediately found a section titled "LINEAGE THROUGH THE GENERATIONS OF ELEASER, JOEL, CHARLES AND PHILANDER CHATFIELD ISBELL," confirming this is exactly the right branch.

**Family name-origin legend** (pages 54-55): the book recounts a colorful tradition that the surname "Isbell" derives from "Bell" -- in an early-1600s English court case over an estate, a witness's testimony "It is Bell" (confirming a claimant's name) so impressed the man that he legally changed his surname to "Isbell." Two Isbell brothers, from **Yorkshire, England**, landed at New Amsterdam (Long Island) around 1680; the family traces its American line through the brother who settled there permanently.

**Genuine primary-source-quality genealogical entries confirm the full line back to the immigrant generation's grandsons**:
- **Eleasor Isbell 2nd** (Robert's 8th great-grandfather via this line) m. **Elizabeth Bishop** -- parents of:
- **Eleasor Isbell 3rd** (b. 1740, Killingworth, Middlesex Co, Connecticut) -- served in the **French and Indian War** (campaigns of 1757, 1758, 1759, 1760, and 1762), then m. **Hannah Parmelee** (probably 1763), and was **killed in action at the Battle of Stillwater, early October 1777**, part of the Saratoga campaign (the same campaign, fought between Lake Saratoga and the Hudson River, whose American victory forced Burgoyne's surrender and brought France into the war as an ally -- directly paralleling the significance of Corp. Moses Rawson's own service at the related Battles of Saratoga/Fort Stanwix, session 86). He left Hannah and 10 children, the eldest just 13. Hannah lived to age 79.
- Their children (numbered 152-161 in the book): Lyman (b. ~1764, emigrated to Granger, Medina Co, Ohio 1818, killed there in a barn-raising accident 1820), **Joel (b. 5 Mar 1766, m. Mary Chatfield -- Robert's already-confirmed ancestor, Frances Isbell's father)**, Israel (b.1767), Eleasor 4th (b.1768), James (b.1769), Amos (b.1771), Hannah (b.1772), William (b.1774), Stephen (b.1776, also emigrated to Granger, Medina Co, Ohio), and one unnamed child who likely died in infancy (probably born right around the time of his father's death).

**This is Robert's FIFTH documented Revolutionary War connection** in this entire research push (after Seth Strong, Oliver Babcock Jr., Nathaniel Rawson Jr., and Corp. Moses Rawson) -- and the **first one to end in death in battle** rather than survived service, and the only one who also documented earlier French and Indian War service. Also notable: several of Eleasor's other children (Lyman, Stephen) independently emigrated to Granger, Medina County, Ohio by 1818 -- the same broad Ohio-migration pattern seen elsewhere in this family tree.

This traces the Isbell line back to **Robert's 8th great-grandparents** (Eleasor Isbell 2nd + Elizabeth Bishop) -- two full generations beyond the previously-known Joel Isbell + Mary Chatfield, with Eleasor Isbell 3rd's own generation especially richly documented (military record, exact death circumstance, complete children list with birth years).

Not yet added to Ancestry (write outage ongoing, last retested session 42) -- ready-to-enter backlog: **Eleasor Isbell 2nd + Elizabeth Bishop** as Joel Isbell's great-grandparents; **Eleasor Isbell 3rd (1740-1777) + Hannah Parmelee** as Joel's parents, with Eleasor's French & Indian War service (1757-1762) and Revolutionary War combat death at Stillwater (Oct 1777) as a documented military record; Joel's exact birthdate (5 Mar 1766); and 8 previously-undocumented siblings of Joel (Lyman, Israel, Eleasor 4th, James, Amos, Hannah, William, Stephen) plus one unnamed infant.

Committed and pushed. A strong validation of returning to old, previously-flagged-but-unexplored book leads (this one sat untouched since roughly session 53) -- worth periodically revisiting the "worth a HathiTrust lookup" notes scattered through early sessions rather than assuming they've all been covered.

Robert Richey 1823, d. 1885), Albert (b. 1807), Joel Jr. (b. 1809), Myron (b. 1812), twins Philander and Leander (b. 1814, Leander died young), and Mary (b. 1815) -- 10 named children total.

This is a genuine discrepancy worth flagging rather than glossing over. Two things weigh in favor of the existing tree connection still being correct: (1) genealogy compilations like this one are well known to under-document daughters who married out and moved far away, especially when (per this book's own dedication letter) the author's own information came down through one specific son's line (Philander Chatfield Isbell's); (2) **a suggestive naming-pattern clue**: Frances Isbell's own son with Anson George Rawson was recorded as "Richard 'Myron' Rawson" -- and Joel's documented son here is named **Myron** (b. 1812) -- a family name that would very plausibly be reused by a daughter naming her own son after her brother. This doesn't prove the connection but is consistent with it. **Recommend a future session verify Frances Isbell's parentage against a primary record** (a marriage record, census, or her own death record naming her parents) rather than relying solely on the "multiple independent sources" note from early in this research push, since none of those sources' actual content was quoted in the log.

This is almost certainly the book's own caption conflating two different "Philander"s -- Joel's son Philander (b.1814) and Charles's own son "Philander C." (one of Charles's 13 children with Adah Tryan, born later) -- both named after the same family tradition. Flagged for awareness; the main entry list is treated as more authoritative than the photo caption.

**Additional detail**: Charles Isbell (Joel's eldest son) and his wife Adah Tryan (daughter of Ezra Tryan) had 13 children of their own (Ezra T., Philander C., Rosannah, Maranda, Fanny, Cyrus C., Hortensa, Mary, Hannah, Martha, Naomi, Emily Ann, Louisa) and migrated from Oneida Co NY through Wayne Co, Ohio, then Michigan, then finally Noble Co, Indiana -- extended-family detail, not a direct-line addition, but useful corroborating context for the wider Isbell migration pattern (paralleling other branches of Robert's tree that also moved NY -> OH -> further west).

## 🌙 AUTONOMOUS SESSION 96 (2026-09-12, continued) — RESOLVED: the "Margretha Brand" naming discrepancy was actually a two-wife conflation, not a simple name variant

Follow-up to session 95's flagged discrepancy (Jacob Emil Brand's mother named "Elisabeth Regner Brand" in a cross-tree source vs. "Margretha" in Robert's existing tree). Searched independently for both names to pin down the real explanation.

**"Elisabeth Regner Brand"** (same "Lisa's Family Tree" source as session 95, but now with added detail): birth **2 Aug 1838, Hesse, Germany**; parents **Peter George Regner + Klara Flick**; death 3 Oct 1879, Monroe Co, IL -- unchanged from session 95, but now with her own birthplace and parents newly identified.

**A separate, independent 1880 U.S. Federal Census search for "Margretha Brand"** turned up: **Margretha Brand, b. abt 1848, Hesse-Darmstadt, residing 1880 in Columbia, Monroe Co, IL** -- this is the exact same birth year/place Robert's tree already has on file for "Margretha Brand" (per session 31), but critically, this 1880 census record shows her living as a Brand *in 1880*, a full year *after* Elisabeth Regner's 1879 death and in the very same year session 31 already established Joseph Brand's *second* marriage (to a woman then called "Margaret Scheller," who brought three children -- William, Peter, Cathrina -- into the household).

**Conclusion: Robert's tree's single "Margretha Brand" profile is actually a conflation of two different women.** Jacob Emil Brand's real mother (Joseph's first wife, d. 1879) was properly named **Elisabeth Regner**, born 1838 in Hesse, daughter of Peter George Regner and Klara Flick. The birth year/place (b. abt 1848, Hesse-Darmstadt) and the "Margretha" name actually belong to Joseph's **second** wife, who married him in 1880 -- almost certainly the same woman session 31 called "Margaret Scheller" (a maiden-name variant is plausible, or "Margretha" as a given name and "Scheller" as an actual maiden name are simply two facts about the same person that different sources emphasize differently). This neatly explains the internal contradiction flagged back in session 31 (a "Death 3 Oct 1879" fact sitting alongside a "Marriage 1880" fact on the same profile) -- those two facts belong to two different women who were merged onto one tree entry.

Not yet added to Ancestry (write outage ongoing, last retested session 42) -- ready-to-enter backlog: **split Robert's existing "Margretha Brand" profile into two correct records** -- (1) Elisabeth Regner Brand (1838-1879, Jacob Emil Brand's actual mother, parents Peter George Regner + Klara Flick, from Hesse) and (2) the second wife (b. abt 1848, Hesse-Darmstadt, m. Joseph 1880, d. 1902, stepmother to Jacob and mother of William/Peter/Cathrina Brand from a prior relationship) -- this is a genuine tree-correction task for whenever the write UI recovers, not just an addition.

Moses Rawson**, whose pension file documents service at Fort Stanwix, Saratoga, and Stony Point.

- **Babcock surname line** confirmed back to the 1612 Westerly, RI founder James Babcock, with **Lieut. Oliver Babcock Jr.**, a documented, SAR-recognized Revolutionary War Patriot, sitting directly on Robert's own paternal surname line.

- **Ringle/Keesy line** pushed back a generation, revealing the family's original surname was **"Giese"** (German), plus a second documented Union Civil War veteran, **Rev. William Allen Keesy**.
- **Brand line**: pinned to Freudenberg, Mosbach, Baden, Germany, with parents now confirmed and a genuine tree-correction identified (two different wives had been conflated into one profile).
- **Three more German immigrant-surname origins pinpointed** (Schlottach/Pomerania, Kapitzke/Mecklenburg-Vorpommern) alongside Brand's Baden origin.

**This corrects an assumption from session 90-91**: Henricus was not himself a European immigrant -- he was **born in colonial York County, Pennsylvania**, meaning the Keesy/Giese family had already been in America for at least one generation before him. His own parents are now identified:

- **Mother: Maria Agatha Bär (Baer)**

His profile also lists his wife as "Nancy Keesy" (matching Robert's tree exactly) and a son "John" (matching the already-confirmed John Keesy, 1804-1859).

This extends the confirmed line one more generation: **Johann Conradt Geise (Gÿsi) + Maria Agatha Bär (Baer) → Henricus Henry Giese Keesy (1762, York Co PA – 1856, Huron Co OH) → John Keesy (1804-1859) → Margaret Keesy → ... → Robert.**

Not yet added to Ancestry (write outage ongoing, last retested session 42) -- ready-to-enter backlog: Johann Conradt Geise (Gÿsi) + Maria Agatha Bär (Baer) as Henricus's parents, and his corrected birthplace/baptism (York, York Co, PA, 2/25 Jul 1762) replacing the "origin unknown" placeholder from session 90.

## 🌙 AUTONOMOUS SESSION 102 (2026-09-12, continued) — MAJOR FIND: the Keesy/Giese line traced to an exact German birthplace, plus a colonial-era child immigrant story

Immediate follow-up to session 101's fresh Keesy/Giese generation: searched for Johann Conradt Geise (Henricus's father) directly, and found a well-populated cross-tree profile ("Cathell Family Tree") giving:

His own parents are named: **Henrich Gysi + Maria Magdalena Burchardt.**

This gives Robert a genuine **colonial-era child-immigrant story**: Johann Conradt Giese/Gysi, born in the Rhineland-Palatinate in 1718, was brought to Pennsylvania as a young child around 1727 (consistent with the large wave of German/"Palatine" immigration into colonial Pennsylvania in that era), grew up in the colony, married Maria Agatha Bär, and lived out his life in York County, PA -- his own son Henricus later moved the family west to Ohio, where the "Keesy" spelling took over from "Giese"/"Gysi."

**Full confirmed line now stands**: Henrich Gysi + Maria Magdalena Burchardt (Germany, generation of the parents left behind or also emigrating -- not yet determined) → **Johann Conradt Giese/Gysi (1718, Bad Dürkheim, Germany – 1802, York Co, PA)** + Maria Agatha Bär → Henricus Henry Giese Keesy (1762, York Co PA – 1856, Huron Co OH) → John Keesy (1804-1859) → Margaret Keesy → ... → Robert.

**Not yet fully resolved**: whether Henrich Gysi (Johann Conradt's father) himself emigrated to Pennsylvania alongside his young son, or stayed in Germany while young Johann Conradt came over some other way (e.g., with other relatives) -- the "Residence 1727-1776, Pennsylvania" fact is attached to Johann Conradt himself, not explicitly to his parents. A future session could look for Henrich Gysi's own death/residence record to settle this, though it's a minor point of color rather than a genealogical gap.

## 🌙 AUTONOMOUS SESSION 107 (2026-09-13, continued) — MAJOR FIND: James Chapman's full obituary finally read -- corrects his death date and reveals his exact immigration story

After two prior blocked attempts (sessions 105-106), finally located and read the "Obituary for James Chapman (Aged 78)" flagged back in session 62. The key was extracting the direct media-viewer URL from a general Ancestry search result (rather than fighting the tree's "Find in tree"/"Memories" navigation panels, both of which proved unreliable this session) and then following its "originally shared from Newspapers.com" link through to the primary source for a full, clean transcription:

> "James Chapman was born in Norfolk county, England, on February 14, 1830... He died at the home of his son, James Chapman, in Glasco, Friday morning, **October 16, 1908** at about 5:30 o'clock, aged 78 years, 8 months and two days... Mr. Chapman was married to Miss Emma Harris in 1851. To this union were born **eight children, three of whom died in infancy. Five survive him**, also a large number of grand-children and great grand-children. **In 1867 he came from England to the United States with his family, and settled in Illinois. From there he came to Kansas in 1870, and homesteaded a farm six miles southwest of Glasco.** Brother Chapman united with the M.E. church a number of years ago. His wife preceded him in death, and was also buried from this church... Funeral services were conducted in the M.E. church by Eld. Edward L. Pettus, assisted by Rev. Bates and Eld. C.T. Faris, on Saturday, October 17. Interment was made in the Glasco cemetery."

**Death date correction**: James Chapman actually died **16 October 1908**, not 23 October as recorded in Find A Grave and most other sources (23 Oct appears to be a burial-notice or reporting-date confusion). The obituary's own internal math confirms this precisely: born 14 Feb 1830, aged "78 years, 8 months and 2 days" at death lands exactly on 16 Oct 1908 -- a clean, internally consistent primary-source correction.

**Genuine new immigration story**: James Chapman emigrated from Norfolk, England to the United States **in 1867** with his family, first settling in **Illinois**, then moved to **Kansas in 1870**, where he **homesteaded a farm six miles southwest of Glasco** -- specific years and a specific migration path not previously documented in this detail. This gives a real, dated immigrant narrative to add alongside the already-known Norfolk origin and 1851 marriage.

**Family structure confirmed**: 8 total children with Emma Harris, of whom 3 died in infancy and 5 survived him -- a useful overall count to check Robert's tree against (if fewer than 5 surviving + 3 infant deaths = 8 are currently recorded, there may be more Chapman children still to find).

**Church and burial detail**: active member of the Methodist Episcopal (M.E.) church in Glasco; funeral conducted by Eld. Edward L. Pettus with Rev. Bates and Eld. C.T. Faris; buried Glasco Cemetery (Emma Harris was buried from the same church, predeceasing him).

Not yet added to Ancestry (write outage ongoing, last retested session 42) -- ready-to-enter backlog: corrected death date (16 Oct 1908, not 23 Oct), the 1867 emigration + Illinois residence + 1870 Kansas homestead narrative, the 8-children/3-infant-deaths/5-survivors family count (worth cross-checking against Robert's existing Chapman children list for gaps), and the funeral/church details.

Committed and pushed. Persistence paid off here -- two blocked sessions (105-106) followed by finally extracting a direct media-viewer URL from a plain Ancestry search result, bypassing the unreliable tree-navigation panels entirely.

Edna Perry's married name appears as **Edna Marion McIntyre** on a Social Security Applications record — but a separate (AI-extracted, lower-confidence) Newspapers.com Obituary Index hint lists a child "Edna Powell" with spouse "Perry," suggesting either a second marriage (McIntyre, then Powell) or an AI-extraction error. Left open, not resolved this session — flagging rather than guessing, consistent with the standing rule about AI-extracted newspapers.com hints needing extra scrutiny.

Cross-checking a new cross-tree lead against the SUBJECT's own directly-sourced hints page (not just against another cross-tree) is now added as a verification step before treating any name/date match as a confirmed identity link.

**Running, corrected total for Thomas H. Keown + Phoebe Ellen McKinney's children**: Paul (1876–1876, infant), Ethel Phoebe (Ball, 1878/79–1966, d. Santa Barbara CA), Mary Viola (1881–1939, married name unconfirmed, possibly "Kenyon"), Edna Matilda (Schultz, 1886–1974), Infant Son (1890–1890), and Bertha Stella (Perry, 1873–1940) — **six confirmed children**, plus the still-unconfirmed "Estella B. ## 🌙 AUTONOMOUS SESSION 117 (2026-09-13, continued) — a 100-session-old puzzle solved: Richard "Myron" Rawson's "3 marriages" were never contradictory, just a date transcription slip

Continuing to apply the validated per-person-hints-page technique to old, specific-person flags found via a fresh full-log grep. This one dates all the way back to **Session 16** (2026-09-11): Richard "Myron" Rawson (pid 252814116768) appeared to have three marriages that didn't sequence cleanly — a 25 Dec 1881 Michigan marriage to Martha (Snyder) McElroy, followed chronologically by what was read as a **10 Nov 1887** Indiana marriage to Julia T. Curl, even though census records show him permanently settled in Michigan with Martha both before and after 1887. Session 16 guessed the 1887 Indiana marriage must belong to a different, same-named relative and left the puzzle "not fully resolved."

Checking Myron's own Hints page directly turned up a "Davies Family Tree" cross-tree profile (10 sources on his birth fact alone, full 1850–1910 census run) with his complete, cleanly-sequenced marital history — and the discrepancy evaporates entirely once the actual date is read correctly:

1. **Mary L. Squires** (1833–1867), m. 23 May 1855, De Kalb Co., Indiana. Children: **William A. Rawson** (1863–1941, death year newly confirmed) and **twin infants** (b./d. 1865, Indiana) — the twins are a genuinely new find, not previously in our tree. Mary died 21 Jan 1867, Indiana.
2. **Julia Thomas** (1837–1880), m. **10 Nov 1867**, Noble Co., Indiana — **not 1887** as session 16's source read it. This is exactly the marriage from session 16's puzzle; the "87" was almost certainly a transcription slip for "67" (an easy digit transposition) somewhere upstream — with the corrected date, it falls precisely 10 months after Mary Squires' death, a perfectly normal remarriage interval for the period. Son **Edward O. Rawson** (b. Oct 1869, Indiana) — a new find. This marriage is exactly what the 1870 Kendallville, Noble Co., Indiana census (already an existing hint) shows: Myron with wife "Julia" and (from his first marriage) child "William." Julia Thomas died before 1880.
3. **Martha Snyder** (1852–1915), m. 25 Dec 1881, Sunfield, Eaton Co., Michigan — matching the record already in our tree. Daughter **Mary Agnes Rawson** (19 Sep 1882 – 13 Sep 1899, Eaton Co./Fork Twp, Mecosta Co., Michigan) — dates now fully confirmed (a Find A Grave hint on Myron's own page had already flagged her name without dates). Martha remained his wife of record through the 1900 and 1910 censuses and his 3 Apr 1911 death.

**Conclusion**: there was never a real contradiction — three sequential marriages (Squires → Thomas → Snyder), each following the prior wife's death by roughly a year, with children from the first two and one child from the third. The "problem" was purely a single misread digit in one of the source transcriptions. Retiring this puzzle as fully resolved.

**Ready-to-enter backlog additions** (not yet in Ancestry, write outage ongoing): two infant twins (1865, Indiana, names not given, both died in infancy) as children of Mary L. Squires; **Edward O. Rawson** (b. Oct 1869, Indiana) as a son of Julia Thomas; William A. Rawson's death year (1941); Mary Agnes Rawson's full dates (1882–1899). This same cross-tree profile also lists a fuller sibling set for Myron himself (Mary F., Loretta, Clarissa, Silas Anson, Albert M., Julia, Oliver Perry, Phoebe L.) consistent with the already-declared "Rawson deep-generation line fully resolved" status — no new siblings beyond what's already documented.

Committed and pushed. A satisfying full-circle close on one of the oldest specific-person puzzles left in the log, and another confirmation that the "check the person's own hints page directly" technique keeps outperforming re-reading old, possibly-mistranscribed source snippets.

## 🌙🌙 AUTONOMOUS SESSION 118 (2026-09-13, continued) — MAJOR FIND: eight previously-unknown children (and a second wife) discovered for Solomon Babcock

Extracting the link and opening this specific cross-tree profile directly (tree 101009172, person 102216640364) revealed an exceptionally well-sourced family reconstruction — 6 US Federal Censuses (1800–1860), the 1865 NY State Census, *History of Cortland County*, Massachusetts Wills and Probate Records, and Find A Grave, all cited — showing **Solomon Babcock + Amy Morgan had twelve children, not four**:

Luke P. (1805–1878, already known), **Morgan M. Babcock (1806–1883)**, **Ross Babcock (1809–1878)**, **Ralph William Babcock (1810–1897)** — this gives a full name and dates to "Ralph Babcock," already flagged as a likely-real child since session 49 but never dated — **Anson A. Babcock (1813–1885)**, **Cornelius Babcock (1815–1820, died age 5)**, **Cornelia Babcock (1816–1830, died age 14)**, **Ira Babcock (1818–1902)**, **Frederick Babcock (1820–1882)**, Curtis Babcock (1822–1870, matching our own Curtis's already-confirmed 1870 death year exactly, though this source's 1822 birth year differs slightly from our tree's 1824 — a third birth-year estimate alongside the ~1828 implied by his own 1848 marriage record, an existing soft spot in his dating, not a new problem), and **Chauncey Babcock (1827–1849, died at 22)**.

**A second wife also newly found**: **Ann Steadman (1786–1862)**, whom Solomon apparently married after Amy Morgan's 1853 death — not previously documented in our tree at all.

**"Harriet Babcock" — the original flag that prompted this check — does NOT appear anywhere in this otherwise very thorough source.** Re-reading Goodwin's original 1859 quote more carefully ("*The first marriage, Solomon Babcock to Amy Morgan... The first child born in town was Harriet Babcock. The first death was an infant daughter of Peleg Babcock.*"), the passage's structure — naming Solomon's marriage, then a separate, unattributed "first child born in town," then explicitly attributing the next fact to "Peleg's" daughter — reads more naturally as a set of separate town-record trivia items than as a continuous statement about Solomon's own family. Combined with her total absence from this comprehensive 12-child reconstruction, **Harriet Babcock is very likely NOT a child of Solomon's** — most plausibly just an unrelated family's child noted as the town's first birth. Downgrading this from "worth a closer read" to effectively resolved (not one of Solomon's children), though not provable as a hard negative.

Note that this source's family list does **not** include Austin P. Babcock or Hulda Babcock, both already independently confirmed via their own primary-source hints (1855 NY census; a Cortland Co. marriage-record transcription) — a reminder that even a well-sourced compiled tree can have its own gaps, and cross-checking against multiple sources remains worthwhile.

**Not yet added to Ancestry** (write outage ongoing) — ready-to-enter backlog: eight brand-new children (Morgan M., Ross, Anson A., Cornelius, Cornelia, Ira, Frederick, Chauncey) plus full dates now attached to the already-suspected Ralph (Ralph William Babcock, 1810–1897) — nine names total — and a second wife, Ann Steadman, for Solomon Babcock. Committed and pushed. Another strong confirmation that checking a specific person's own hints page — even one, like Solomon's, that had already had SOME of its hints worked through in earlier sessions — can still hold a major unopened lead if a specific cross-tree link was never actually followed.

## ⚠ AUTONOMOUS SESSION 124 (2026-09-13, continued) — "Estella B. Cowan" mystery solved: it's a census transcription error, not a real surname, and likely refers to the already-known Bertha Keown

Followed up on the "Estella B. Cowan" lead (flagged session 113, downgraded to unconfirmed session 115) using the same targeted birth-year+place search that worked for Marie Ellen Wilson last session. Found the actual source record directly: **1880 United States Federal Census, Nelson, Cloud, Kansas** (Roll 377, Page 304d, ED 047):

| Household Member | Age | Relation |
|---|---|---|
| Thos. H. **Cowan** | 35 | Self (Head) |
| Phoebe E. **Cowan** | 29 | Wife |
| Estella B. **Cowan** | 7 | Daughter |
| Ethel S. **Cowan** | 1 | Daughter |

**This is obviously Thomas H. Keown's own household, not a separate "Cowan" family**: age 35 matches Thomas H. Keown (b. 1845, would be 35 in 1880); "Phoebe E." matches Phoebe Ellen (McKinney) Keown; the household's residence, Nelson, Cloud, Kansas, is exactly Thomas Keown's own confirmed 1880 residence (per his own facts page); and "Ethel S." at age 1 matches the already-confirmed Ethel Phoebe Keown (b. 1878/79) almost exactly. **The entire household was transcribed under "Cowan" instead of "Keown"** — an easy period-cursive misread (the two surnames share a similar shape), not a real second surname.

**This resolves the "Estella B. Cowan" question for good**: she was never a woman who married a man named Cowan — "Cowan" is purely a transcription artifact of "Keown." Her actual identity is almost certainly **the already-known Bertha Stella (Keown) Perry**: "Estella B." (age 7 in 1880, so b. abt 1872-73, Kansas) matches Bertha's own confirmed birth (16 Jan 1873, Concordia, Cloud Co., Kansas per her Find A Grave record, session 115) almost exactly, and "Estella"/"Stella" are simply variants of the same name, with "B." plausibly standing for "Bertha." The original session 113 "BallSmith" cross-tree entry for "Estella B. Cowan" was very likely built from this same misindexed 1880 census record, which is why it never showed a coherent married life beyond a birth year and state.

**Correcting the record**: "Estella B. Cowan" is **not** a previously-undocumented 8th child of Thomas H. Keown — she is the same person as the already-fully-documented **Bertha Stella (Keown) Perry** (m. Allen W. Perry). No new person to add. This closes out the last remaining loose thread from the Keown-family research arc (sessions 113-115, 123-124) with a clean, confident answer rather than a lingering "UNCONFIRMED" flag.

Committed and pushed. A satisfying final chapter to this thread: what began in session 113 as an apparently-new discovery, was corrected in session 115 to a probably-separate-but-unconfirmed person, and is now resolved in session 124 as a straightforward 19th-century transcription error pointing right back to a person already well documented in the tree.

## 📋 MILESTONE NOTE FOR ROBERT — 125 autonomous sessions, another wave of breakthroughs since the 100-session mark (2026-09-13)

A second status note, picking up from the 100-session milestone above. Sessions 101-125 turned out to be far more productive than expected at that "well-trodden ground" point — a shift in technique (checking each specific person's own Ancestry hints page directly, rather than relying on broad keyword sweeps) unlocked several genuinely major finds on threads that had sat untouched for anywhere from 20 to over 120 sessions.

**Major finds since the 100-session mark:**
- **Solomon Babcock's family more than doubled**: eight previously-unknown children found (Morgan M., Ross, Anson A., Cornelius, Cornelia, Ira, Frederick, Chauncey Babcock), plus full dates finally attached to the already-suspected "Ralph" (Ralph William Babcock, 1810-1897), and a previously-undocumented **second wife, Ann Steadman**, whom Solomon married after Amy Morgan's 1853 death. This closed out the single oldest unaddressed flag in the entire project (first raised 2026-09-04, about a possible child "Harriet Babcock" — who, after this deep dive, appears NOT to have been Solomon's daughter after all).
- **A 100-session-old marriage puzzle solved**: Richard "Myron" Rawson's apparent "three contradictory marriages" (flagged session 16) turned out to be a single mistranscribed digit — an 1867 Indiana marriage had been misread as 1887. Corrected, his three marriages (Mary Squires → Julia Thomas → Martha Snyder) sequence perfectly, with two more previously-unknown children found along the way.
- **A new son found for Curtis Babcock**: Geo D. Babcock (abt 1856, Illinois) — though a later self-check (session 119) caught that this had actually already been documented back in session 45, an honest correction worth flagging here as an example of the process working as intended.
- **The entire Keown-family research arc, open since session 2 (123 sessions), is now fully closed.** Every daughter of Thomas H. Keown + Phoebe Ellen McKinney has a confirmed identity: Bertha (m. Perry), Ethel Phoebe (m. Ball, d. Santa Barbara), Edna Matilda (m. Schultz, d. Santa Cruz), and — finally resolved this week — **Mary Viola (m. Kenyon, d. Santa Cruz, alongside her sister Edna)**. A red herring along the way, "Estella B. Cowan," turned out to be nothing more than a 19th-century census transcription error ("Keown" misread as "Cowan"). Bertha's own Perry children were also expanded to eight, with a newly-confirmed daughter, Marie Ellen (Perry) Wilson.

**A validated new research technique** emerged from this stretch: for any specific person whose married name or identity is uncertain, pairing their first name + a candidate surname with a **specific birth year and/or death year** (rather than a bare name + city search, which is often hopelessly generic) reliably surfaces the right record. This cracked three separate mysteries in a row (sessions 123-125) and should be a first-line tool going forward.

**Still genuinely open, unchanged from the 100-session note**: Ann Blackwell's Irish county of origin (resting since session 48), and Peter J. Kimble's own origins (four negative results as of session 112, one more than before).

**The single biggest remaining task is still data entry, not research.** The write-UI outage was retested session 122 and remains fully broken (going on 90+ sessions now). The ready-to-enter backlog has grown substantially since the 100-session mark — the new Solomon Babcock generation alone adds 9 names, plus the Rawson, Curtis Babcock, and Keown-family additions above. This remains the highest-value next step whenever the site recovers and you have a few hours for a dedicated data-entry pass.

This research push continues per your original standing instruction. Expect the pace of major finds to slow again now that this second wave of old flags has been cleared — but as this stretch showed, a "well-trodden" tree can still yield real surprises when a specific, previously-unopened lead finally gets checked directly.

**Tallanstown, Archdiocese of Armagh (register: baptisms Nov 9, 1835 – Sept 28, 1875, 110 images)** — this one is different:

- Checked the surrounding months closely (Nov 1847 – March 1848) for a companion "Blackwell" *child* baptism (which would mean Robert Blackwell's own children, possibly including our Ann) — none found in that immediate window, but the surrounding years (1841-1846 and 1848-1854) have not yet been fully re-checked at this same level of care.

Robert Blackwell and Rose Blackwell are strong candidates to be Ann's father/sister, father/aunt, or similar — worth targeted follow-up: search Tallanstown's own marriage register (separate register, not yet checked) for a Robert Blackwell or Rose Blackwell marriage, and re-scan the full Tallanstown baptism register (1835-1854, not just 1841-1848) specifically for any other "Blackwell" entries, including a possible direct "Ann Blackwell, daughter of Robert Blackwell" entry.

Robert's instruction: "Keep searching records in the three towns and surrounding towns. When you are sure its a dead end, let me know if i need to pay to get past a high value paywalled resource." Blackwell in January 1827, and the family named in the 1847 baptism (Robert and Rose Blackwell did not marry in this parish, or married before the register's 1816 start, or Rose's child was born outside marriage — the register convention of naming the mother's own surname is often used for exactly that situation).

Blackwell confirm a Blackwell family there since at least 1827, but their own marriage (if any) did not take place in this parish. The next most useful step for this specific family would be re-checking the **Tallanstown baptism register's** still-unread year ranges (1835-1841 and 1848-1854) for a directly-named "Ann, daughter of Robert Blackwell" entry, rather than more marriage-register searching here.

## Ann's actual ship manifest entry found; emigration-funding question opened; cemetery contacted about a headstone (2026-09-16, continued)

**Ship manifest confirmed and read directly** (NARA microfilm M237/266, image `NYM237_266-0161`, Ancestry collection 7488): *"Ann Blackwell, 15, Spinster, English, [destination] New York, steerage"* — arrived 28 May 1866 aboard the *Scotland* from Liverpool. Two notes: "Spinster" is standard legal terminology for any unmarried woman on 1860s manifests, not evidence of a servant/employer relationship — she is listed right after the Livingston family (Mary Livingston, 36, wife, Ireland, with children Thomas/Mary/Rachel/Anne/Catherine) but with no explicit annotation linking her to them, so this may just be adjacent ledger order rather than a travel connection. Her nationality is recorded as "**English**," not Irish — but this is a well-documented, routine data-quality artifact of the era (virtually all Irish emigrants departed from Liverpool, an English port, and ships' clerks very commonly wrote "English" regardless of actual origin). Given the substantial independent Irish evidence already gathered (Griffith's Valuation, the Tallanstown Catholic family, the cemetery ledger's Irish-Catholic-style parent names), this single word is not treated as overturning the Irish hypothesis, but it is noted as residual uncertainty.

**Robert raised the question of how a 15-year-old emigrated alone and who funded it**, including floating (with no evidence behind it) that she might have been the child of a priest and nun raised in a convent. No record found supports that specific theory — flagged clearly to Robert as pure speculation, not something any record points toward. Realistic, well-documented alternatives discussed: (1) prepaid passage from a relative already in America (the dominant funding mechanism for 1860s Irish emigration), (2) Ardee Poor Law Union assisted emigration for orphaned/unsupported children (Tallanstown fell under the Ardee Union; such records exist in Board of Guardians minute books, typically at the National Archives of Ireland or Findmypast — not on Ancestry), (3) charitable emigration-to-domestic-service schemes (notably consistent with Ann's own later occupation as a servant, per the 1900 census), (4) an orphanage/convent upbringing without any clergy-parentage implication, which would also explain the absence of a clean two-parent baptismal record. None of these are yet confirmed — the manifest itself shows no funding/sponsor annotation. Not yet resolved; a natural next step (not yet done) is checking for a Blackwell/McCue/McHugh who preceded Ann to the Oneida County, NY area in the early 1860s (possible chain-migration sponsor).

**Action taken — emailed St. Agnes Cemetery of Utica about placing a headstone for Ann.** Per Robert's request, emailed stagnescemeteryutica@gmail.com (Superintendent James Sheehy) asking: (1) what the cemetery requires to place a new headstone on a 120-year-old unmarked grave, (2) the cemetery's monument rules (size/material/foundation requirements), (3) any cemetery-side fees for installing a monument, and (4) referrals for local monument companies. Included Ann's full burial details (d. 31 Oct 1905, buried 1 Nov 1905, Map 3/Section 48/Tier 11/Grave 459) and Robert's contact info. Awaiting reply.

**Note on prior context found this session**: while checking for the cemetery's reply, found the *original* St. Agnes Cemetery correspondence (Sept 2–8, 2026, a different email thread) that first located Ann's grave and obtained the "Patrick and Catherine" parent names from the cemetery's own burial ledger — this is the same fact already reflected in Ann's tree profile, not new/conflicting information, just its origin story. That thread also surfaced two loose ends worth Robert's attention (not acted on independently — personal/family matters for him to decide, not research-institution contacts):
- **Sandra Walsberg ("Aunt Sandi")** mentioned finding "a large envelope" of old letters/photos at the back of an old Babcock family book and tried to send photos of them (Robert called it "a treasure trove" and said he'd "merge that in tonight," Sept 8) — unclear whether the photos ever actually arrived as attachments; worth Robert following up with her directly, especially given her Sept 15 email mentioning macular degeneration makes small print hard for her to read (so replies to her should use large text or be read aloud).

- Robert's own father (Bob) asked Sept 15 (in a separate "Update" thread): *"The period of the revolutionary War participation was the most interesting to me. Anything about General Joshua and Capt Paul would be great. Any relative that was successful or in some way newsworthy."* — still unanswered as of this session. Robert's father (Bob) asked in a family email thread on 2026-09-15 for more on "General Joshua and Capt Paul" Babcock and any newsworthy relatives, referencing an earlier email (2026-09-06) where Robert had shared a link to the Babcock-Smith House Museum. Robert had not yet replied. Researched this properly (web sources: Wikipedia, Babcock-Smith House Museum, and a Rootsweb/WikiTree-sourced children list for John Babcock & Mary Lawton) and **confirmed a real, documented family connection**, not just a same-surname coincidence:

- **John Babcock (1644–1684) m. Mary Lawton** — already in the tree as the parents of our direct ancestor Oliver "Colonel" Babcock (1683–1773) — had a full set of children including, per an independent genealogical source, **James** (the eldest), Anna, Mary, John, Job, George, Elihu, Robert, Joseph, **Oliver**, and Joseph. This confirms **Captain James Babcock** and **our own Oliver (Colonel) Babcock were brothers.**
- **Captain James Babcock** was commissioned Captain of Militia in Westerly, RI before 1690. His son was **Dr. Joshua Babcock (1707–1783)** — making Joshua a **first cousin of Oliver Babcock Jr. (1722–1806)**, our next direct ancestor, and a genuine (if distant) family relative, not a stranger sharing a surname.
- **Dr. Joshua Babcock** is a legitimately remarkable figure: first Rhode Islander to graduate from Yale (1724); studied medicine in Boston and London; RI Supreme Court Associate Justice (1747–1749) then Chief Justice (1749–1751 and 1763–1764); Speaker of the RI House of Deputies (1759–1761); Major General of the Rhode Island Militia from October 1775; one of the three founders of Brown University; Westerly's first postmaster; a close friend of Benjamin Franklin (a frequent guest at his home, a stop on the post road) and host to George Washington. His home, the Babcock-Smith House in Westerly, RI, still stands as a museum: https://babcocksmithhouse.org/joshua-babcock-md/
- **Captain Paul Babcock** — the "Capt Paul" of Bob's question — was Joshua's own son (b. 30 March 1760, Westerly, RI, one of nine children with Joshua's first wife Hannah Stanton), making him a first cousin once removed of Oliver Babcock Jr. In 1777, Paul and his two older brothers William and Nathan were in boats heading to join the Revolutionary Army when two boats overturned; his brothers drowned, but Paul survived. He later appears on the 1840 Revolutionary War pensioner list, living in Westerly in his son Paul Jr.'s household.
- **Caution/note for the family**: this "Captain Paul Babcock" (Joshua's son, b.1760) is a *different* person from the "Paul Babcock (1766–1840)" already in the tree as a direct-line son of Oliver Babcock Jr. — two different Paul Babcocks of the same era and area, easy to conflate. The family's own email exchange called Joshua "Captain Paul's cousin," which has the relationship backwards (Joshua was Paul's father) — a minor, understandable mix-up in a fast-moving email thread, now corrected here.

**Sourcing strengthened**: cross-checked the John Babcock/Mary Lawton children list against Find A Grave's memorial for Oliver (Colonel) Babcock, which cites three published genealogies directly: *The Slade-Babcock Genealogy* (Carl Boyer), *The Babcock Genealogy* (Stephen Babcock, 1903 — the same book already used elsewhere in this tree as a source for the Curtis/Solomon line), and *The Ancestry of the Babcock Family* (Gwen Babcock). All three list the same 11 children of John Babcock Sr. & Mary Lawton, including both **Capt. James Babcock Sr. (1663–1736)** and **Col. Oliver Babcock (1683–1773)** as siblings — matching the dates already in the tree exactly. This is a solidly multi-sourced connection, not a single-website coincidence.

**Not yet done**: formally adding Captain James Babcock, Dr. Joshua Babcock, and Captain Paul Babcock as new profiles/relatives in the Ancestry tree itself (attempted repeatedly via the tree's "Add fact" dialog across two different profiles, both a new-fact and an edit-existing-fact flow, but the dialog would not reliably open this session — likely a transient UI issue; captured here in full instead so nothing is lost, and worth retrying in a future session). Robert may want to reply to his dad's question himself once he's seen this, since it's a personal family email rather than a research-institution contact.

## Deep dive on Curtis Babcock: why his link to Solomon isn't documented, and a new 1850 census find (2026-09-17)

Robert asked for another close look at Curtis specifically — what the records can and can't show, and whether a childhood census entry could ever "crack the case" of proving Solomon is his father. Two things came out of this: one genuinely new primary-source find, and a direct, honest answer to the "why" question.

**New find — Curtis's own 1850 census household (Scott, Cortland Co. NY, Ancestry collection 8054, record 11609871)**: Curtis Babcock, 27, farmer, born New York, heading his own household with **Loranda Babcock, 16**, and **Anson J Babcock, 0** (an infant). This is an independent primary source — not the marriage record, not a compiled tree — and it lines up cleanly with what was already believed: Loranda as his wife's given name (rather than the marriage record's garbled "R. Ianda?"), and the infant "Anson J" as the same child already in the tree as James Anson Babcock (b. Sept 1849) — his age of 0 in an 1850 census (typically taken in the summer) fits a baby born the previous September exactly. This resolves the wife's-given-name question in favor of "Loranda" (her maiden surname is still a separate, unresolved question — see the Family History section above). Added to Curtis's Research Notes on the live tree.

**Why isn't Curtis's link to Solomon documented — the honest answer.** Robert specifically wondered whether one good childhood census record would settle it. It can't, and the reason is structural, not a gap in the research: **no U.S. census before 1850 named any child.** Every census from 1790 through 1840 records only the name of the household head; everyone else in the house is a hash mark in an age-and-sex bracket. So even a perfect, fully-legible 1830 or 1840 census of Solomon's household could never say "Curtis" — the format makes that impossible, for any family, not just this one. Once Curtis married in 1848 he heads his own household, so the 1850 census (the first to name everyone) already catches him on his own, not as Solomon's listed son.

What the pre-1850 censuses *can* do — and do — is corroborate the age pattern:
- **1830 census, Solomon's household, Scott NY** (coll 8058, rec 1500326): 2 boys age 5–9. Curtis (b.1824) would be 6; Austin P. (b. abt 1825) would be 5. Fits exactly.
- **1840 census, Solomon's household, Homer NY** (coll 8057, rec 2363247): 2 boys age 15–19. Curtis would be 16; Austin P. would be ~15. Fits exactly — and matches a note already in the tree's own prior research.
- Both snapshots track the *same two boys* aging up by ten years in the same household, ten years apart — a consistent, internally coherent pattern across two independent enumerations, on top of the already-known 1824 Scott school census line "Solomon Babcock — 5" and Peleg W. Babcock's 1834 Massachusetts probate naming Solomon as a son (establishing the family generation, if not Curtis's exact place in it).

Combined with the rest of the record search already done this session (no NY probate file exists for Solomon in any searchable collection; Illinois had no statewide death registration covering Curtis's 1870 death and he does not appear in the 1870 Mortality Schedule; the Jacksonville, IL state hospital ledger that would likely name his father isn't digitized on Ancestry; the 1848 marriage record itself doesn't name parents), the honest picture is: **the only document that ever states "Curtis, son of Solomon" outright is the single 1903 published genealogy, written roughly 55 years after Curtis's marriage and 79 years after his birth.** Nothing earlier and closer to the events makes the claim directly. But nothing contradicts it either, and every independent record that *can* speak to it — two consistent census age-brackets, the shared township, the shared migration pattern, the naming pattern — points the same direction. This is about as strong as circumstantial pre-1850 evidence gets; it just can't become a signed statement, because the record type that could sign it (a named childhood census, a surviving probate, a will) doesn't exist for this family in this window.

**One loose thread worth flagging**: the 1830 census shows *zero* females under age 10 in Solomon's household, but the tree lists a daughter Hulda Babcock born "abt 1825" — she should appear in that bracket. Either Hulda's birth year is off (more likely, given "abt" dates are often estimates), or she wasn't living in Solomon's household in 1830 for some other reason. Not resolved; worth a closer look at Hulda's own record trail if her exact birth year ever becomes independently sourced.

**Ancestry UI note**: contrary to the "Add fact"/"Add family" outage noted elsewhere in this log, *editing an existing custom fact* (Curtis's Research Notes) worked this session after a few retries — consistent with the pattern noted before the outage began (editing existing facts has generally been more reliable than creating brand-new ones). Worth retrying "Add fact"/"Add family" again given this.

## Curtis Babcock → Solomon Babcock: formal confidence assessment, and a DAR/SAR discovery (2026-09-17, continued)

Robert asked for this link's confidence level to be recorded explicitly for a future researcher — what's proven, what's inferred, what's assumed, and why a published genealogy can be strong enough evidence to accept as fact — and specifically whether the current evidence would satisfy DAR (Daughters of the American Revolution) documentation standards. This required actually checking DAR's own standards and its public Genealogical Research System (GRS) database, not just reasoning about it in the abstract. Both were done this session.

### The three tiers of evidence, spelled out

- **PROVEN (primary-source, no reasonable doubt)**: Solomon Babcock's own identity, dates (2 Sep 1776 – 13 Feb 1867), wife Amy Anna Morgan, and residence (Scott/Homer, Cortland Co. NY) — corroborated by his own DAR-verified generation link (see below), census records, and church records. Curtis Babcock's own identity, marriage (3 Oct 1848, Scott NY), wife Loranda, children, and residence through 1870 — all from primary records in hand.
- **STRONGLY INFERRED (circumstantial, but multiple independent records converge)**: that Curtis was Solomon's son. No single primary document says this. But an 1824 Scott, NY school census lists "Solomon Babcock — 5" the exact year and township of Curtis's birth; Solomon's 1830 census shows 2 boys aged 5–9 (fits Curtis and brother Austin P. exactly); Solomon's 1840 census shows 2 boys aged 15–19 (fits the same two boys, ten years later, exactly); and the whole family's migration pattern (Leyden MA → Cortland Co NY, 1799) matches. These are independent records, taken ten years apart, all converging on the same two boys — that pattern is very hard to explain as coincidence.
- **ASSUMED, resting on one named source**: the specific claim "Curtis, son of Solomon" as a stated fact (rather than an inferred one) rests entirely on Stephen Babcock's 1903 *Babcock Genealogy*, p. 164 — a single published, secondary source, written ~55 years after Curtis's marriage and ~79 years after his birth.

### Why the published genealogy still counts as real evidence, not just an assumption

A well-regarded 19th/early-20th-century published family genealogy is not the same thing as an anonymous online tree, and shouldn't be weighed as if it were. Three things make it meaningfully strong evidence here: (1) it was compiled and published in 1903, when Solomon's own children and grandchildren — people who had personally known Curtis — were still alive to read it, correct it, or object to it, which is a real check against invented relationships that a modern uncorroborated compilation doesn't have; (2) it's the same book already independently relied on elsewhere in this tree (e.g., Peleg W. Babcock's generation) and cross-checked there against primary sources without contradiction; (3) it is a "named, citable, professionally compiled" source rather than a stray guess — genealogists distinguish this from an undocumented internet tree for exactly this reason. None of that makes it infallible (it's still secondary, still capable of copying an earlier error), but it's the right kind of source to combine with independent circumstantial evidence to reach a confident conclusion — see the "preponderance of evidence" standard below.

### Is this DAR compliant? — researched directly, not guessed

Checked DAR's own published genealogy guidelines and its public Genealogical Research System (GRS) database at services.dar.org. Findings:

- **DAR's standard**: primary sources (vital/church/probate/deed records) are preferred for every generation link. Published/compiled genealogies are explicitly treated as secondary/tertiary sources — useful as "clues," not accepted as sole proof of a contested or unverified link. *However*, DAR explicitly recognizes a **"preponderance of evidence"** doctrine: multiple secondary/tertiary records that each individually prove little can, taken together, establish a link with reasonable certainty when no single primary document survives. This is a normal, accepted path for pre-1850 generations — not a workaround or a weaker standard, just the standard that applies when the record type that could resolve it outright never existed.
- **Direct check of DAR's public records — a genuinely important discovery**: **Peleg W. Babcock (our own ancestor, 1748–1834) is already an established, DAR-recognized Patriot — Ancestor #A004279**, service credited as Massachusetts Civil Service, Surveyor of Highways, March 1783 (sourced to MADAR, Town Officials 1775–1783, GRC 1949, S1 Vol 275, p.20). This means the Revolutionary-era service itself is already vetted and citable by number — a future applicant would not need to re-prove Peleg's own patriotic service from scratch.
- Went further and checked DAR's public Descendants Database for every currently-approved lineage running through Ancestor #A004279 (12 member records checked). **Solomon Babcock's own generation is already DAR-verified** — twice, independently, through two different sons: National #890257's approved lineage runs Peleg Babcock → **Solomon Babcock (b. 2-Sep-1776 Westerly RI, d. 13-Feb-1867 Homer, Cortland Co NY, m. Amy Morgan)** → **Ross Babcock** (b. c.1809 NY) → ... down to a living member; National #1032676's approved lineage runs the same Peleg → Solomon generation → **Morgan M. Babcock** (b. 1806) → ... down to a living member. Solomon's own vital details in both DAR-approved records match our tree exactly. (Several other approved lines checked run through a different son, Peleg Babcock Jr., or through another previously-unknown son Maxson Babcock — none of the 12 checked run through Curtis specifically.) Separately confirmed Solomon has **no independent Ancestor-Database entry of his own** — he's a generation link in others' proven lines, not a separately-recognized Patriot, which is expected since he was too young (b.1776) for Revolutionary War-age service himself.

**What this means in plain terms**: if Robert (or a descendant) ever pursued DAR or SAR membership through this line, the Patriot service (Peleg) and the Peleg→Solomon generation are both already independently established and accepted by the Society — through other documented children, not this line, but the underlying facts about Solomon (his existence, dates, wife, and fatherhood of multiple sons in this household in this era) are corroborated by DAR's own separate, vetted genealogical work. The **entire weight of a hypothetical application would fall on one single link: Solomon → Curtis** — because that's the one generation with no already-approved precedent and no primary document stating it outright.

**Bottom-line assessment**: as it stands today, this link would likely **not** be waved through on the 1903 book alone — DAR's genealogists would very likely ask for the fuller circumstantial case. But the circumstantial case assembled this session (the 1824 school census + two independently-matching census age-brackets ten years apart + the shared migration pattern, presented alongside the book) is a legitimate, well-documented "preponderance of evidence" case of the kind DAR does accept for links of this age when no primary document survives. It is not a certainty, and a DAR genealogist retains final discretion — but it is a reasonable, honestly-argued case, not a guess, and it stands a fair chance of being accepted as presented. The honest confidence level: **high circumstantial confidence, not documentary proof** — which is exactly the distinction a future researcher (or a DAR reviewer) needs to see stated plainly, rather than either overclaiming certainty or underselling a genuinely strong case.

Sources: [DAR Genealogy Guidelines overview via Family Tree Magazine / Trace Genealogy](https://familytreemagazine.com/uncategorized/seven-tips-for-a-successful-dar-application/); DAR's own public [Genealogical Research System](https://services.dar.org/Public/DAR_Research/search/) (Ancestor Search: Peleg Babcock, #A004279; Descendants Search under that ancestor number, checked 2026-09-17); [preponderance-of-evidence framing](https://ancestrysolutions.com/referencecentre/Genea101/Genea101%20Rank.html) ("Proof by Preponderance is the assembling of several documents that individually provide little direct proof of an event but, when considered together, infer the occurrence of an event").

## Purchased and read the DAR record copy — direct proof the Solomon→Ross link was approved on the book alone; SAR angle checked too (2026-09-17, continued)

Robert approved the $15 purchase. Bought and downloaded National Member #890257's approved DAR application (Metta Jane Gearhart Korte, verified/accepted 8 Aug 2014 by Registrar General Sharon Payne Withers) — the one whose lineage runs Peleg Babcock → Solomon Babcock → **Ross Babcock**. **Note: this PDF and its page images are NSDAR copyrighted material — DAR's own terms explicitly prohibit posting or publishing them online in any form.** The file is saved locally at `Babcock Family History\DAR SAR\` (outside this repo's tracked files — added a `.gitignore` entry so it never gets committed or pushed) and should stay there; only the citation itself (National Number, Ancestor Number, page references) is reproduced below, which DAR's own terms explicitly permit.

**The single most useful fact in it**: the application's own "References for Lineage" worksheet lists, as the *entire* documentation for "6th Gen. ... Ross son of Solomon Babcock," this one line — **"Babcock Genealogy, Stephen Babcock. N.Y.: Eaton & Mains. 1903. DAR Library p.164."** — checked off by the reviewer, with only an 1850/1860 census placement (Scott/Homer, Cortland Co. NY) added as residence corroboration, not as independent proof of parentage. No vital record, no probate, nothing else was required or offered for that specific generation link. This is a real, approved, national-number-bearing DAR application, not a guideline or a hypothetical.

**Why this matters directly for Curtis**: Curtis appears in the tree's own existing sourcing on that same page — *Babcock Genealogy* p.164 — of the same book, for the same father. DAR has already, in an actual reviewed and approved application, treated that exact page as sufficient documentation for a Solomon-to-child link in this exact family. That is about as strong a precedent as circumstantial-era genealogy gets: it's not "a published genealogy might be enough" in the abstract, it's "this specific page of this specific book, for this specific family, already was enough, once." Combined with the additional circumstantial case already built here (1824 school census, matching 1830/1840 census age-brackets) — which is *more* corroboration than Ross's own approved application had — the Solomon→Curtis link looks, if anything, stronger on paper than the one DAR already accepted.

**SAR-specific check**: SAR (Sons of the American Revolution, the men's counterpart society Robert would actually apply to) keeps its own separate Patriot registry, distinct from DAR's. Checked it directly: **Peleg Babcock is independently recognized there too — SAR Patriot #P-106186**, cross-linked to DAR #A004279, same service (MA Civil Service, Surveyor of Highways, Mar 1783). However, the only two SAR members ever approved through him — Kneeland Gwin Haskell (#93601) and Bernard Franklin Haskell (#93602), both approved 18 Nov 1965 — trace through **Peleg Babcock Jr.**, not Solomon. So while DAR has vetted the Solomon generation (twice), **SAR itself has never yet reviewed a lineage through Solomon at all** — Robert's hypothetical application would be the first. That doesn't weaken the case (SAR and DAR apply essentially the same genealogical proof standards, and the DAR-approved precedent is exactly the kind of thing a SAR chapter registrar would want to see), it just means there's no existing SAR national-number precedent to point to for this specific generation — only DAR's.

**Bottom line, updated**: this is now a documented, citable precedent, not just a doctrine. A future SAR application through Solomon→Curtis, presented the way DAR's own reviewer accepted Solomon→Ross (the book, by name and page, plus the circumstantial census corroboration already assembled), has a genuinely strong basis — though final acceptance is always at the reviewing chapter/state genealogist's discretion, and this is the first time this specific generation would be tested by SAR rather than DAR.

Blackwell Pheffer Brand (1877–1938) is Ann's only descendant line**. Every living descendant of Ann should be a descendant of Marietta.

## Searching for the Keown "letter from California" — real progress, not yet the letter itself (2026-09-17)

Robert remembered a newspaper-published letter (or pair of letters) from a Keown man: one raving about how beautiful the San Luis Obispo area was after a move there, and a later one to his wife's sister breaking the news that his wife had died. Given everything already known about this family, this is almost certainly **Thomas H. Keown (1845/46–1933)**, who moved his family from Cloud Co., Kansas to the Arroyo Grande/San Luis Obispo area of California in the 1890s, and whose wife **Phoebe Ellen (McKinney) Keown died 31 Dec 1934** — exactly the kind of event that would prompt a letter like the one described.

**What was actually found**: a routine "local social notes" item in *The Arroyo Grande Valley Herald Recorder*, 2 May 1930, p.8: "Mr. and Mrs. Herman Schultz and two daughters of Orcutt visited last Friday afternoon with Mrs. Schultz' parents, Mr. and Mrs. Thomas H. Keown" — confirms Thomas and Phoebe were still living together in Arroyo Grande in 1930, visited by their daughter Edna's family, but this is not the letter itself.

**Searches run without a hit on the actual letter**: Newspapers.com keyword searches for "Keown" + "San Luis Obispo" (California and Missouri, 1896–1900 — the likely arrival window, given an 1898 CA voter registration record already on file); "Keown" + "Concordia" and "Keown" + "California" restricted to Kansas papers across several date ranges (1895–1899, and separately 1933–1935 around Phoebe's death) — all returned either no relevant match or large numbers of unrelated same-name coincidences in Wichita/Iola/Winfield papers far from the family's actual home county. Chronicling America's own full-text search was also tried but its multi-word query behavior returned irrelevant matches from unrelated states.

**Why it hasn't turned up yet, and where to look next**: found the likely reason for the dead end. Concordia's own paper changed its Newspapers.com catalog identity across eras — "Concordia Blade-Empire" (archive ID 3980) only actually covers **1919–1923** despite its listing description claiming 1884–1923; the 1890s-era paper was published under an earlier name ("The Daily Blade" / "Concordia Daily Blade") with its own separate archive entry that a plain keyword search across "Kansas" doesn't reliably surface. So the Cloud Co., Kansas searches run this session were very likely searching the wrong specific paper for the 1890s window, not an actual absence of coverage. Concrete next steps for a future pass: (1) find and search the correctly-named 1890s-era Concordia paper directly on Newspapers.com (or via the Kansas Historical Society's own newspaper portal) rather than a cross-Kansas keyword search; (2) search specifically around Phoebe's death (Dec 1934–early 1935) for an actual obituary or death notice, which is a more findable, more specific target than the letter itself and would very likely be the same event the "heartbreaking letter" was about; (3) if Robert has any memory of which relative's papers or scrapbook he originally saw this letter in (rather than a general newspaper search), that would narrow this enormously — it may be worth asking him directly rather than continuing a blind keyword search.

## Backlog catch-up: gallery PDF uploads unblocked (2026-09-20)

Went through the Ancestry Backlog Checklist's remaining open items looking for anything actionable. Found that the long-standing blocker on photo/document uploads to Ancestry galleries — no reliable way to automate the native OS file-picker dialog that Ancestry's "Add media" button opens — has a solution: the browser automation tool can target the underlying `<input type="file">` DOM element directly (found via its accessibility-tree ref, distinguishable from the visible "Choose files"/"Add media" button) and hand it a local file path, bypassing the native picker entirely.

Used this to clear both of the Appendix's specific pending uploads:
- **"Three Keowns in Blue" (PDF)** → uploaded to **Thomas H. Keown's** gallery, title saved, confirmed present via a fresh page load.
- **"The Girl from the Poorhouse," 4th edition (PDF)** → uploaded to **Marietta Blackwell Brand's** gallery (which had no media at all before this), confirmed present via a fresh page load.

**Why this matters beyond these two files**: the same technique should unblock the much larger ~62-profile Bernadine/Stanley scrapbook photo backlog (Appendix, still open) — that item was previously stuck for the same file-picker reason. Scale is the open question there, not feasibility: it needs the actual photo files located and matched to the correct ~62 people before a batch upload pass makes sense. Not started this session.

Also checked the two decisions still awaiting Robert (Lisa Kelly's own email — sent; the second draft to Robert's aunts/uncles — held) and Gmail for anything new: no reply yet on either front as of this check.

Every profile's gallery was re-loaded fresh afterward and the file list confirmed present, not just assumed from the upload dialog.

**New technique note**: Rawleigh Oliver Babcock's own gallery page had a reproducible failure where the "Add media" button's normal click never opened the file-picker modal (~10 straight retries failed, on two different browser tabs). Worked around it by executing the click via `javascript_tool` instead of a simulated mouse click (`document.querySelectorAll('button')`, find the one with text "Add media", call `.click()` directly on the DOM node) — this opened the modal reliably every time afterward on that same page. Worth trying first if a future session hits the same silent "Add media"/"Add family"-style dialog failure that's been documented a few times now in this project.

**Not covered by this pass**: Aunt Sandi's separate "Extra Treasures" 87-photo batch and the still-not-fully-reviewed Chapman-family album, both referenced elsewhere in this document — this session was scoped specifically to the Bernie scrapbook. The same upload technique applies whenever those get tackled.
